`CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md`:

```md
# Contributor Covenant Code of Conduct

## Our Pledge

We as members, contributors, and leaders pledge to make participation in our
community a harassment-free experience for everyone.

## Our Standards

Examples of behavior that contributes to a positive environment:

* Demonstrating empathy and kindness toward other people
* Being respectful of differing opinions and experiences
* Giving and gracefully accepting constructive feedback
* Focusing on what is best for the community

## Enforcement

Report unacceptable behavior to the maintainers via the security contact in
[SECURITY.md](./SECURITY.md).

## Attribution

This Code of Conduct is adapted from the [Contributor Covenant](https://www.contributor-covenant.org),
version 2.1.

```

`CONTRIBUTING.md`:

```md
# Contributing to MobShield

Thank you for your interest in MobShield. This project spans `mobshield-android`, `mobshield-ios`, and `mobshield-spec`.

## Getting started

1. Read [MOBSHIELD_SPEC.md](../mobshield-spec/MOBSHIELD_SPEC.md).
2. Pick a platform directory and build the sample app.
3. Open a draft PR early for architectural changes.

## Pull requests

- Keep changes focused; one concern per PR.
- Add or update tests for Kotlin/Swift logic you change.
- Native detection changes must document new signal names in `mobshield-spec/DETECTION_CATALOG.md`.
- Run local linters before pushing (ktlint/detekt on Android, SwiftLint on iOS).

## Licensing

All source files, including the native C/C++ code, are licensed under **Apache 2.0**. See `LICENSE`.

## Code of conduct

See [CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md](./CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md).

```

`README.md`:

```md
# MobShield Android

Open-source mobile app hardening for Android: modular RASP detectors, signal aggregation, and per-build native personalization.

[![Android CI](https://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android/actions/workflows/android-ci.yml/badge.svg)](https://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android/actions/workflows/android-ci.yml)
[![Maven Central](https://img.shields.io/maven-central/v/io.github.inforaam/mobshield-core)](https://central.sonatype.com/artifact/io.github.inforaam/mobshield-core)
[![License: Apache-2.0](https://img.shields.io/badge/license-Apache--2.0-blue.svg)](LICENSE)
![minSdk: 24](https://img.shields.io/badge/minSdk-24-lightgrey.svg)

MobShield runs a set of detection modules, aggregates their weighted signals into `ThreatEvent`s, and reports your app's runtime posture through a listener — so your app can react to root, hooking, debugging, emulator, and integrity-tampering conditions.

## Why MobShield

- **Signals, not a verdict.** Each check emits a weighted, confidence-scored `Signal`. A pure aggregator turns them into a `ThreatEvent` per threat type (root, hooks, debugger, emulator, integrity), so you decide what a score means for your app instead of getting an opaque pass/fail.
- **Modular.** Take only the detectors you need: `mobshield-core` plus any of the root, hooks, debugger, environment and integrity modules.
- **Detect-only by default.** MobShield reports; it never kills your process unless you opt in with a `TerminationPolicy`.
- **No account, no telemetry.** There is no backend and no outbound network calls; the only socket use is the Frida check, which probes `127.0.0.1` on the device. Reports stay in your app; send them to your own server if you want them (see [docs/report-attestation.md](docs/report-attestation.md)).
- **Modern-root aware.** Checks target Magisk, Zygisk, Shamiko and KernelSU artifacts, and Frida and LSPosed instrumentation, not just a `su` file lookup.
- **Auditable.** Every signal, its technique and its default weight is documented in [docs/detection](docs/detection), with a clean-vs-compromised [validation matrix](docs/validation.md). Coverage is mapped to OWASP MASVS in [docs/masvs-mapping.md](docs/masvs-mapping.md).

### When to use it (and when not to)

- **Use it** for client-side posture signals you can act on or forward to your backend: warn, restrict a feature, step up authentication, or flag a session.
- **Pair it with server-side checks.** Client-side detection can be bypassed by a determined attacker with control of the device. Treat MobShield as one layer of defense in depth, and verify anything that matters on your server (for example with [Play Integrity](https://developer.android.com/google/play/integrity) attestation). It does not replace it.
- **Native detectors are best-effort.** Several checks read `/proc` and probe the runtime, which is the same ground an attacker's tooling operates on. Expect cat-and-mouse; keep the library updated.

## Modules

| Module | Purpose |
|--------|---------|
| `mobshield-core` | Public API facade, aggregator, validation harness, JNI bridge (skeleton) |
| `mobshield-detect-root` | Magisk, Zygisk, Shamiko, KernelSU signals |
| `mobshield-detect-hooks` | Frida, LSPosed, Xposed |
| `mobshield-detect-debugger` | ptrace, TracerPid |
| `mobshield-detect-environment` | Emulator, automation, ADB |
| `mobshield-detect-integrity` | Signature and build anchor |
| `mobshield-gradle-plugin` | Per-build personalization (`io.mobshield.personalize`) |
| `mobshield-sample-app` | Compose integration demo (all detectors) |

## Why MobShield

- **Fully auditable.** The detection logic is open source, so you can review it, fork it, and audit it. There's no binary blob.
- **No telemetry.** MobShield makes no network calls. Threat events go only to your listener and, if you choose, your backend.
- **Modular.** Add only the detectors you need.
- **Safe to roll out.** It's detect-only by default and never kills your process unless you opt in.
- **Harder to bypass generically.** The Gradle plugin personalizes each build, so one off-the-shelf bypass script doesn't fit every app.
- **Server-verifiable.** Posture reports answer a challenge from your server and are signed by a hardware-attested key (or bound to a Play Integrity verdict), so your backend can check where a report came from and that it is fresh.

MobShield raises the cost of an attack. Like any client-side protection, it can't make an app unbreakable, so pair it with server-side checks.

## How it compares

| | MobShield | [freeRASP](https://github.com/talsec/Free-RASP-Android) | [RootBeer](https://github.com/scottyab/rootbeer) |
|---|---|---|---|
| License | Apache-2.0 | MIT wrapper + proprietary binary core, fair-usage policy | Apache-2.0 |
| Detection logic auditable | ✅ | ❌ (closed binary) | ✅ |
| Sends data to a vendor | ❌ No network calls | Incident data feeds Talsec's weekly Security Report | ❌ |
| Root (Magisk, Zygisk, Shamiko, KernelSU) | ✅ | ✅ | Basic (su binary, root/cloak apps, props) |
| Hooking (Frida, LSPosed, Xposed) | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| Debugger | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| Emulator / automation / ADB | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| Signature / repackaging integrity | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| Backend-verifiable report | ✅ Key Attestation or Play Integrity, your own server | AppiCrypt (commercial plan) | ❌ |
| Per-build personalization | ✅ | ❌ | ❌ |
| Platforms | Android + [iOS](https://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-iOS) | Android, iOS, Flutter, RN, Cordova, Capacitor | Android |

freeRASP is ahead on cross-platform framework support and a few extra checks, such as installation source and screen capture. If you need those today, it's a good choice. *Comparison based on each project's public README as of October 2026. Corrections are welcome via PR.*

## OWASP MASVS mapping

| MASVS v2 control | MobShield module |
|---|---|
| MASVS-RESILIENCE-1: platform integrity | `mobshield-detect-root`, `mobshield-detect-environment` |
| MASVS-RESILIENCE-2: anti-tampering | `mobshield-detect-integrity` |
| MASVS-RESILIENCE-4: anti-dynamic analysis | `mobshield-detect-hooks`, `mobshield-detect-debugger`, `mobshield-detect-environment` |

## Requirements

- JDK 17+
- Android SDK 34
- Gradle 8.7+ (wrapper included)
- NDK **28.0.13004108** (via SDK Manager; required for **16 KB page size** / Play compliance)

Native modules are built with `ANDROID_SUPPORT_FLEXIBLE_PAGE_SIZES=ON` and 16 KB ELF linker alignment. Rebuild and publish a new tag after upgrading the NDK.

## Install

### Maven Central (recommended)

MobShield is published to Maven Central under the `io.github.inforaam` group. Add `mobshield-core`, plus only the detectors you need:

```kotlin
dependencies {
    val mobshield = "1.0.10"
    implementation("io.github.inforaam:mobshield-core:$mobshield")
    // optional detectors:
    implementation("io.github.inforaam:mobshield-detect-root:$mobshield")
    implementation("io.github.inforaam:mobshield-detect-hooks:$mobshield")
    implementation("io.github.inforaam:mobshield-detect-debugger:$mobshield")
    implementation("io.github.inforaam:mobshield-detect-environment:$mobshield")
    implementation("io.github.inforaam:mobshield-detect-integrity:$mobshield")
}
```

The `io.mobshield.personalize` Gradle plugin is not part of the Maven Central release, because its plugin id sits outside the `io.github.inforaam` namespace.

### JitPack (single umbrella dependency)

```kotlin
// settings.gradle.kts -> dependencyResolutionManagement.repositories
maven { url = uri("https://jitpack.io") }

// build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    implementation("com.github.Inforaam:MobShield-Android:1.0.8")
}
```

JitPack builds from git tags, so it offers the tagged releases (latest: 1.0.8). The umbrella artifact is a single AAR that bundles core and all five detect modules, and it cannot be trimmed to individual modules; use Maven Central for that.

## Quick start

Register the detectors, implement a listener, and start MobShield with an application `Context`. It runs in **detect-only** mode by default (it reports; it never terminates your process).

```kotlin
import io.mobshield.core.MobShield
import io.mobshield.core.MobShieldConfig
import io.mobshield.core.MobShieldListener
import io.mobshield.core.ThreatEvent
import io.mobshield.detect.root.RootDetectionRegistrar
import io.mobshield.detect.hooks.HookDetectionRegistrar
import io.mobshield.detect.debugger.DebugDetectionRegistrar
import io.mobshield.detect.environment.EmulatorDetectionRegistrar
import io.mobshield.detect.integrity.IntegrityDetectionRegistrar

val listener = object : MobShieldListener {
    override fun onThreat(event: ThreatEvent) {
        Log.w("MobShield", "threat: ${event.type} ${event.severity} score=${event.score}")
    }

    override fun onAllChecksFinished(events: List<ThreatEvent>) {
        Log.i("MobShield", "scan finished: ${events.size} threat(s)")
    }
}

val config = MobShieldConfig() // detect-only by default
RootDetectionRegistrar.register(context, config)
HookDetectionRegistrar.register(context, config)
DebugDetectionRegistrar.register(context, config)
EmulatorDetectionRegistrar.register(context, config)
IntegrityDetectionRegistrar.register(context, config)
MobShield.start(context, config, listener)
```

Read the current posture at any time, or stop scanning:

```kotlin
val state = MobShield.getState() // riskLevel, activeThreats, running, lastScanMs
MobShield.stop()
```

Common configuration (all optional):

```kotlin
val config = MobShieldConfig.builder()
    .periodicIntervalSec(30)                       // rescan every 30s (default: one scan at start)
    .expectedPackageId("com.example.app")          // anchor package-id integrity
    .expectedSigners(listOf("<sha256-hex>"))       // anchor signing-cert integrity
    .detectOnly(false)                             // opt in to termination
    .terminationPolicy(TerminationPolicy.EXIT_ON_CRITICAL)
    .build()
```

Before turning termination on, read [docs/termination.md](docs/termination.md): termination needs a
signal that is strong on its own, `terminateOn` limits which threat types can end the process, and a
hooked app can block the exit call, so termination is a deterrent rather than a security boundary.

For a posture report your backend can verify, see [docs/report-attestation.md](docs/report-attestation.md):
`MobShield.currentAttestedReport(challenge)` answers a challenge from your server with a report signed
by a hardware-attested key. The HMAC-signed `currentSignedReport(key)` is deprecated, because its key
ships in the app.

## Build

```bash
./gradlew :mobshield-sample-app:assembleDebug
./gradlew test
```

See [mobshield-sample-app/README.md](mobshield-sample-app/README.md) for a walkthrough.

## Documentation

- [Detection validation matrix](docs/validation.md) — every signal each module emits and the clean vs compromised baselines.
- [Detection notes](docs/detection) — per-detector background.
- [Scoring model](docs/scoring.md) — how signals become scores and severities, correlation groups, what one signal can do alone, and the limits of the default weights.
- [Process termination](docs/termination.md) — when a termination policy ends the process, how to keep a threat type report-only, and why termination is not a security boundary.
- [Verifying reports on your server](docs/report-attestation.md) — server challenges, Key Attestation and Play Integrity, what the server must check, and what a verified report cannot prove.

## Release

Pushing a `v*.*.*` tag makes that release installable through JitPack, and runs [`release.yml`](.github/workflows/release.yml), which stages the six library modules in the Sonatype Central Portal. The deployment is published by hand from the portal after it has been checked. The workflow needs the `maven-central` environment and these repository secrets: `MAVEN_CENTRAL_USERNAME`, `MAVEN_CENTRAL_PASSWORD` (a Central Portal user token), `SIGNING_KEY` (ASCII-armored GPG private key), `SIGNING_KEY_ID` and `SIGNING_KEY_PASSWORD`.

Local verification:

```bash
./gradlew :mobshield-core:publishToMavenLocal -PVERSION_NAME=1.0.7-SNAPSHOT
```

All of MobShield Android (Kotlin, Gradle and native C/C++ sources) is licensed under [Apache-2.0](LICENSE).

`SECURITY.md`:

```md
# Security Policy

## Supported versions

The latest tagged release is supported. Security fixes are made on `main` and released as a new tag.

| Version | Supported          |
| ------- | ------------------ |
| Latest release (see [Releases](https://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android/releases)) | :white_check_mark: |
| Older releases | :x: |

## Reporting a vulnerability

**Please do not open public GitHub issues for security vulnerabilities.**

Use GitHub's private reporting: go to the repository's **Security** tab and choose
[**Report a vulnerability**](https://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android/security/advisories/new).

Include:

- Affected module (for example `mobshield-detect-root`, `mobshield-core`)
- Steps to reproduce
- Impact assessment (bypass, crash, privacy)

We aim to acknowledge reports within 72 hours and provide a remediation timeline
within 14 days for confirmed issues.

A detection bypass (a technique that defeats a signal on a device an attacker controls) is
expected to exist for any client-side detector. Reports of one are still welcome, particularly
with a reproduction, so the detector can be improved.

## Safe harbor

Good-faith security research on your own builds of the sample apps is welcome.
Do not test third-party production apps without permission.

```

`build.gradle.kts`:

```kts
import com.vanniktech.maven.publish.MavenPublishBaseExtension
import com.vanniktech.maven.publish.SonatypeHost
import io.gitlab.arturbosch.detekt.Detekt
import io.gitlab.arturbosch.detekt.DetektCreateBaselineTask

plugins {
    id("com.android.application") version "8.5.2" apply false
    id("com.android.library") version "8.5.2" apply false
    id("org.jetbrains.kotlin.android") version "2.0.21" apply false
    alias(libs.plugins.kotlin.compose) apply false
    id("org.jetbrains.kotlin.jvm") version "2.0.21" apply false
    id("io.gitlab.arturbosch.detekt") version "1.23.7" apply false
    id("org.jlleitschuh.gradle.ktlint") version "12.1.1" apply false
    alias(libs.plugins.maven.publish) apply false
    alias(libs.plugins.nexus.publish)
}

val isJitPackBuild =
    System.getenv("JITPACK") == "true" ||
        findProperty("jitpack") == "true"

// JitPack: publish only the fat umbrella AAR (all classes inside). Maven Central: all modules.
val publishableAndroidModules =
    if (isJitPackBuild) {
        setOf("mobshield")
    } else {
        setOf(
            "mobshield",
            "mobshield-core",
            "mobshield-detect-root",
            "mobshield-detect-hooks",
            "mobshield-detect-debugger",
            "mobshield-detect-environment",
            "mobshield-detect-integrity",
        )
    }

tasks.register("jitpackPublish") {
    group = "publishing"
    description = "Build fat MobShield AAR and publish for JitPack"
    dependsOn(
        ":mobshield:mergeReleaseFatAar",
        ":mobshield:publishMavenPublicationToMavenLocal",
    )
}

// Detekt infers JVM target from the running JDK; pin to project target so JDK 21+ local installs still build.
subprojects {
    tasks.withType<Detekt>().configureEach {
        jvmTarget = "17"
    }
    tasks.withType<DetektCreateBaselineTask>().configureEach {
        jvmTarget = "17"
    }
}

// JitPack: set ORG_GRADLE_PROJECT_GROUP in jitpack.yml to com.github.<user>.<repo>
// so transitive module coordinates resolve from the same JitPack publication.
val mobshieldGroup: String = findProperty("GROUP") as String? ?: "io.github.inforaam"
val mobshieldVersion: String =
    // Prefer explicit Gradle property (used by Maven Central CI).
    findProperty("VERSION_NAME") as String?
        // Fall back to repo-local default.
        ?: findProperty("mobshield.version") as String?
        // Allow JitPack to inject a version from its VERSION env var (e.g. tag).
        ?: System.getenv("VERSION")?.removePrefix("v")
        // Final fallback for local development.
        ?: "0.0.0-SNAPSHOT"

subprojects {
    if (name !in publishableAndroidModules) {
        return@subprojects
    }

    pluginManager.apply(rootProject.libs.plugins.maven.publish.get().pluginId)

    val artifactId = name
    group = mobshieldGroup
    version = mobshieldVersion

    extensions.configure<MavenPublishBaseExtension> {
        coordinates(mobshieldGroup, artifactId, mobshieldVersion)

        pom {
            name.set("MobShield $artifactId")
            description.set("MobShield mobile RASP library: $artifactId")
            inceptionYear.set("2025")
            url.set("https://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android")
            licenses {
                license {
                    name.set("Apache License, Version 2.0")
                    url.set("https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0")
                    distribution.set("repo")
                }
            }
            developers {
                developer {
                    id.set("inforaam")
                    name.set("MobShield Contributors")
                    url.set("https://github.com/Inforaam")
                }
            }
            scm {
                connection.set("scm:git:git://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android.git")
                developerConnection.set("scm:git:ssh://git@github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android.git")
                url.set("https://github.com/Inforaam/MobShield-Android/tree/main")
            }
        }

        // Deployments wait in the Central Portal for a manual "Publish" until a first release has been checked.
        publishToMavenCentral(SonatypeHost.CENTRAL_PORTAL, automaticRelease = false)
        if (hasSigningCredentials()) {
            signAllPublications()
        }
    }
}

fun hasSigningCredentials(): Boolean {
    val props = listOf(
        "signingInMemoryKey",
        "signing.keyId",
        "SIGNING_KEY",
    )
    return props.any { rootProject.findProperty(it) != null || System.getenv(it) != null }
}

nexusPublishing {
    repositories {
        sonatype {
            nexusUrl.set(uri("https://ossrh-staging-api.central.sonatype.com/service/local/"))
            snapshotRepositoryUrl.set(uri("https://central.sonatype.com/repository/maven-snapshots/"))
        }
    }
}

tasks.register("clean", Delete::class) {
    delete(rootProject.layout.buildDirectory)
}

```

`docs/detection/android-debug.md`:

```md
# Android Debugger Detection (mobshield-detect-debugger)

This module implements six checks that emit `Signal` values aggregated into `DEBUGGER` and `ADB_ENABLED` threat events (MASVS-RES-7).

## Signal catalog

| Signal | Technique | Default weight | Default confidence |
|--------|-----------|----------------|------------------|
| `android.debug.tracerpid` | `/proc/self/status` TracerPid | 85 | 90 |
| `android.debug.ptrace` | `PTRACE_TRACEME` / EPERM heuristic | 80 | 85 |
| `android.debug.timing` | Monotonic timing anomaly | 65 | 70 |
| `android.debug.app_debuggable` | `FLAG_DEBUGGABLE` in release builds | 70 | 60 |
| `android.adb.enabled` | `ADB_ENABLED` / USB debugging settings | 30 | 40 |
| `android.debug.waiting` | `Debug.waitingForDebugger()` / `isDebuggerConnected()` | 90 | 95 |

`android.adb.*` maps to informational `ADB_ENABLED` (not CRITICAL by default). Other signals map to `DEBUGGER`.

Override weights via `MobShieldConfig.detectionTuning`.

## Native checks (`libmobshielddebug.so`)

### tracerpid_check.cpp

Reads `/proc/self/status` and parses `TracerPid`. Non-zero indicates an attached tracer.

### ptrace_self.cpp

Calls `ptrace(PTRACE_TRACEME, ...)`. Returns detected when attach fails with `EPERM` while another tracer is active.

### native_debugger_timing.cpp

Measures `clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC)` over a short loop; unusually slow iterations suggest debugger single-stepping.

## Kotlin checks

### AppDebuggable.kt

Uses `ApplicationInfo.FLAG_DEBUGGABLE`. Weight is **70** when the app is a release variant (`BuildConfig.DEBUG == false`), **0** in debug variants (no signal).

### AdbEnabledCheck.kt

Reads `Settings.Global.ADB_ENABLED` and `adb_usb_enabled`. Low weight; common on developer devices.

### WaitingForDebugger.kt

Uses `Debug.waitingForDebugger()` and `Debug.isDebuggerConnected()`.

## Registration

```kotlin
DebugDetectionRegistrar.register(context, mobShieldConfig)
```

Module name: `debug`. Module criticality: `100` (scheduling priority; ADB remains informational at aggregation).

```

`docs/detection/android-emulator.md`:

```md
# Android Emulator and Automation Detection (mobshield-detect-environment)

This module implements six checks for QEMU emulators and UI automation frameworks. Signals aggregate to `EMULATOR` and `AUTOMATION` (MASVS-RES-4, MASVS-RES-10).

Weights are intentionally moderate so stock AVDs used by developers typically stay below CRITICAL on default thresholds.

## Signal catalog

| Signal | Technique | Default weight | Default confidence |
|--------|-----------|----------------|------------------|
| `android.env.qemu_props` | `ro.kernel.qemu`, `ro.hardware`, etc. | 22 | 65 |
| `android.env.qemu_device` | `/dev/qemu_pipe`, `qemud` socket | 28 | 75 |
| `android.env.cpu_goldfish` | `Hardware: Goldfish` / `Ranchu` in cpuinfo | 24 | 70 |
| `android.env.build_fingerprint` | `Build.FINGERPRINT` / product heuristics | 20 | 60 |
| `android.env.sensor_count` | `SensorManager` sensor list size | 18 | 55 |
| `android.automation.framework` | Appium / UiAutomator services | 30 | 70 |

Override weights via `MobShieldConfig.detectionTuning`.

## Native checks (`libmobshieldemu.so`)

### qemu_props.cpp

Uses `__system_property_get` for `ro.kernel.qemu`, `ro.hardware`, `ro.product.device`, and `ro.boot.qemu`.

### qemu_devices.cpp

`stat()` on `/dev/socket/qemud`, `/dev/qemu_pipe`, and `/dev/goldfish_pipe`.

### cpu_features.cpp

Parses `/proc/cpuinfo` for Goldfish or Ranchu hardware strings.

## Kotlin checks

### BuildFingerprintCheck.kt

Inspects fingerprint, manufacturer, product, model, brand, and hardware for emulator markers (`sdk_gphone`, `ranchu`, `generic`, etc.).

### SensorCountCheck.kt

Physical devices usually expose more sensors than minimal AVD images. Default threshold: fewer than 7 sensors.

### AutomationFrameworkCheck.kt

Scans running service class names and enabled accessibility services for `io.appium`, `uiautomator`, and `androidx.test`.

## Registration

```kotlin
EmulatorDetectionRegistrar.register(context, mobShieldConfig)
```

Module name: `environment`. Module criticality: `35` (lower than root/hooks/debug; emulator is expected in dev workflows).

```

`docs/detection/android-hooks.md`:

```md
# Android Hook Framework Detection (mobshield-detect-hooks)

Detects Frida, objection, LSPosed, Xposed, and related instrumentation. Module name: `hooks`. Criticality: 100.

## Signal catalog

| Signal | Technique | Default weight | Default confidence |
|--------|-----------|----------------|------------------|
| `common.hook.frida_maps` | `/proc/self/maps` scan | 75 | 85 |
| `common.hook.prologue` | libc prologue baseline + trampoline heuristic | 80 | 90 |
| `android.hook.frida_port` | TCP connect to 127.0.0.1:27042/27043 (100ms) | 85 | 95 |
| `android.hook.thread_name` | `/proc/self/task/*/comm` Frida threads | 70 | 80 |
| `android.hook.art_dex` | JNI class load probe for Xposed/LSPosed | 75 | 85 |
| `android.hook.suspicious_library` | maps vs `System.mapLibraryName` | 55 | 65 |
| `android.hook.xposed` | `ClassLoader.loadClass` for bridge types | 70 | 80 |
| `android.hook.stack_leak` | `Thread.getAllStackTraces()` prefix scan | 65 | 75 |

Tune via `MobShieldConfig.detectionTuning`.

## Native checks (`libmobshieldhooks.so`)

### 1. proc_maps_scan.cpp

Reads `/proc/self/maps` for `frida-agent`, `gum-js-loop`, `linjector`, `XposedBridge.jar`, `/data/adb/lspd`, and related strings.

**Survives:** Frida hide that keeps artifacts in memory maps.

**Bypass:** manual map cleaning, renamed agents.

**Restricted /proc:** returns `UNAVAILABLE` (no signal).

### 2. function_prologue_inspect.cpp

Reads first 16 bytes of `open`, `read`, `syscall`, `android_dlopen_ext` via `dlsym`. Compares against XOR-encrypted baselines keyed by `MOBSHIELD_HOOKS_ENTROPY_SEED_*` from `generateHooksBuildInfo` (overridden by `mobshield-plugin` at app build time). When baselines are unset (`0xFF`), uses ARM64 trampoline heuristic only.

**Survives Frida Stalker:** yes for inline hooks that patch function entry (Stalker does not rewrite prologue in all modes).

**Bypass:** hardware breakpoints only, late inline hooks after baseline capture, ARM64 hook gadgets that preserve first 16 bytes.

### 3. frida_port_probe.cpp

Non-blocking `connect()` to loopback ports 27042 and 27043 with 100ms socket timeout.

**Survives:** network-only hide if server still listens locally.

**Bypass:** custom port, host-only Frida.

### 4. thread_name_scan.cpp

Scans `/proc/self/task/*/comm` for `gmain`, `gdbus`, `gum-js-loop`, `pool-frida`, `linjector`.

**Survives:** renamed threads still often match `gum-js-loop` unless patched.

**Bypass:** thread name spoofing.

### 5. art_inspection.cpp

JNI-only: uses app `ClassLoader.loadClass` for known bridge class names. No private ART struct walking.

**Survives:** Xposed/LSPosed when classes are loadable.

**Bypass:** Shamiko package/class hide; customized package names.

## Kotlin checks

### 6. SuspiciousLibraryScan.kt

Maps file must contain suspicious library lines (frida/xposed/lsposed tokens), not merely `libc.so`.

### 7. XposedModuleQuery.kt

Try/catch `Class.forName` via app `ClassLoader` (no shell).

### 8. ClassNameLeakScan.kt

Inspects stack traces for `de.robv.android.xposed`, `org.lsposed`, `frida`, etc.

## Integration

```kotlin
HookDetectionRegistrar.register(context, config)
MobShield.start(context, config, listener)
```

## Constraints

- No `Runtime.exec` or shell.
- Native JNI calls use 100ms Kotlin `withTimeout` per check.
- Graceful degradation when `/proc` is unavailable (Android 11+ scoped restrictions).
- Encrypted prologue baselines tied to per-build entropy seed.

## Emulator acceptance

Stock Pixel emulator API 34/35 without `frida-server` should produce zero `HOOK_FRAMEWORK` events at CRITICAL severity after aggregation.

`docs/detection/android-integrity.md`:

```md
# Android Integrity Detection (mobshield-detect-integrity)

Kotlin-only v1 module for package signature, install source, APK checksum, and native library checksum verification. Signals map to `APP_INTEGRITY` and `UNOFFICIAL_STORE` (MASVS-RES-2).

## Signal catalog

| Signal | Technique | Default weight | Default confidence |
|--------|-----------|----------------|------------------|
| `android.integrity.signature` | APK Signing Scheme v2/v3 SHA-256 digests | 90 | 95 |
| `android.store.installer` | `getInstallSourceInfo()` installer package | 25 | 45 |
| `android.integrity.apk_checksum` | `PackageManager.requestChecksums` (API 31+) | 85 | 90 |
| `android.integrity.native_lib_checksum` | On-disk SHA-256 of `libmobshieldcore.so` | 80 | 85 |

`android.store.installer` is informational (`UNOFFICIAL_STORE`). Other integrity signals are high weight.

## Checks

### SignatureCheck.kt

`PackageManager.GET_SIGNING_CERTIFICATES` with `SigningInfo.apkContentsSigners`. Compares digests to `MobShieldConfig.expectedSigners`. Skipped when the allowlist is empty.

### InstallerOriginCheck.kt

Uses `PackageManager.getInstallSourceInfo()` (API 30+) and compares `installingPackageName` to `MobShieldConfig.allowedInstallSources` (defaults include Play Store IDs).

### ApkChecksumCheck.kt

On Android 12+, calls `requestChecksums` with `TYPE_WHOLE_SHA256` and compares to `MobShieldConfig.expectedApkSha256`.

### NativeLibChecksumCheck.kt

Opens `nativeLibraryDir/libmobshieldcore.so`, SHA-256 hashes the file, and compares to `MobShieldConfig.expectedNativeLibSha256`.

## Configuration

```kotlin
MobShieldConfig.builder()
    .expectedSigners(listOf("...64-char-sha256..."))
    .expectedApkSha256("...optional...")
    .expectedNativeLibSha256("...optional...")
    .allowedInstallSources(setOf("com.android.vending"))
    .build()
```

## Registration

```kotlin
IntegrityDetectionRegistrar.register(context, mobShieldConfig)
```

Module name: `integrity`. Module criticality: `100`.

`docs/detection/android-root.md`:

```md
# Android Root Detection (mobshield-detect-root)

This module implements nine independent checks that each emit at most one `Signal`. Signals are aggregated by `mobshield-core` into `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS` threat events.

## Signal catalog

| Signal | Technique | Default weight | Default confidence |
|--------|-----------|----------------|------------------|
| `android.root.mount_namespace` | PID 1 vs self mountinfo diff | 75 | 85 |
| `android.root.magisk_uds` | Magisk abstract UDS in `/proc/net/unix` | 70 | 80 |
| `android.root.overlayfs` | tmpfs/overlay/Magisk bind on `/system`, `/vendor`, `/sbin` | 65 | 75 |
| `android.root.errno_deviation` | Unexpected `stat()` errno on blocked paths | 60 | 70 |
| `android.root.zygisk_maps` | Zygisk loader lines in `/proc/self/maps` | 70 | 80 |
| `android.root.kernelsu_sysfs` | KernelSU paths and maps artifacts | 75 | 85 |
| `android.root.path_probe` | `su` file existence (no shell) | 25 | 35 |
| `android.root.dangerous_packages` | Known root/hook manager packages | 55 | 70 |
| `android.root.props` | Anomalous `ro.debuggable`, `ro.secure`, `ro.build.tags` | 20 | 30 |

Override weights via `MobShieldConfig.detectionTuning` keyed by signal name.

## Native checks (`libmobshieldroot.so`)

### 1. mount_namespace_check.cpp

Compares mount points in `/proc/1/mountinfo` vs `/proc/self/mountinfo`. Flags paths visible to init but not the app when they match Magisk, Zygisk, KernelSU, or hidden `/system` bind patterns.

**Survives:** basic `su` hiding, package hiding.

**Bypass:** Shamiko whitelist with full mount namespace concealment; careful per-app Zygisk hiding.

**Emulator note:** whitelists `/apex`, `/metadata`, `/linkerconfig`, `/product` to avoid Pixel AVD false positives.

### 2. magisk_uds_probe.cpp

Parses `/proc/net/unix` for socket names containing `magisk`, `zygisk`, or `@MAGISK`.

**Survives:** hidden manager UI, removed APK.

**Bypass:** renamed daemon socket, socket hiding modules.

### 3. overlayfs_check.cpp

Parses `/proc/self/mounts` for tmpfs or Magisk-tagged overlay mounts on critical partitions.

**Survives:** file-based `su` removal.

**Bypass:** mountless injection; Shamiko mount hiding.

### 4. errno_deviation.cpp

`stat()` on `/data/adb/magisk`, `/data/adb/ksu`, and known `su` paths. Detects existence or unexpected errno (not `EACCES`/`ENOENT`).

**Survives:** package-uninstall-only hides.

**Bypass:** path redirection; Shamiko `/proc` spoofing.

### 5. zygisk_loader_scan.cpp

Scans `/proc/self/maps` for `libzygisk`, `zygisk.so`, or `/zygisk/` paths only (no broad anonymous-RWX heuristic).

**Survives:** Magisk app hide.

**Bypass:** manual map cleaning; inline hooks without loader name.

### 6. kernelsu_check.cpp

Probes `/data/adb/ksu`, `/data/adb/ksud`, and KernelSU strings in maps.

**Survives:** Magisk-only hides.

**Bypass:** path rename; kernel-only mode without artifacts.

## Kotlin checks

### 7. SuPathScan.kt

Uses `File.exists()` on static paths and `PATH` directories. No `Runtime.exec`.

**Survives:** UI-only hides.

**Bypass:** Shamiko path hide; renamed `su`.

### 8. DangerousPackagesScan.kt

`PackageManager.getPackageInfo` for Magisk, KernelSU, Xposed, LSPosed, Substrate package IDs.

**Survives:** `su` deletion.

**Bypass:** Shamiko package hide; repackaged manager names.

### 9. SystemPropertiesProbe.kt

Reads `ro.debuggable`, `ro.secure`, `ro.build.tags` through native `__system_property_get` (no Kotlin reflection). Flags `debuggable=1`, `secure=0`, or `test-keys`.

**Survives:** post-boot prop reset tools (partial).

**Bypass:** resetprop hiding; OEM test images (tune confidence down).

## Integration

```kotlin
val config = MobShieldConfig(
    detectionTuning = mapOf(
        RootSignalDefaults.PATH_PROBE to SignalTuning(weight = 15, confidence = 25),
    ),
)
ModuleRegistry.register(RootDetectionModule(context, config.detectionTuning))
MobShield.start(context, config, listener)
```

## Constraints

- Each native probe is invoked from Kotlin with `withTimeout(50ms)`.
- No shell execution anywhere in this module.
- Acceptance target: no signals on stock Pixel emulator API 34/35 images with default Google Play image.

```

`docs/masvs-mapping.md`:

```md
# MobShield and OWASP MASVS

This page maps what MobShield Android detects to the resilience controls of the
[OWASP Mobile Application Security Verification Standard (MASVS) v2](https://mas.owasp.org/MASVS/),
group **MASVS-RESILIENCE**.

MobShield is a runtime detection library. It supplies *evidence* for these controls; it does not by
itself make an app compliant, and MASVS-RESILIENCE is defense in depth rather than a guarantee. See
[When to use it](../README.md#when-to-use-it-and-when-not-to).

| MASVS control | Intent | MobShield module | Signals (see [detection notes](detection)) |
|---------------|--------|------------------|--------------------------------------------|
| MASVS-RESILIENCE-1 | The app validates the integrity of the platform | `mobshield-detect-root`, `mobshield-detect-environment` | Root: `android.root.*` (mount namespace, Magisk UDS, overlayfs, Zygisk maps, KernelSU). Environment: `android.env.*` (QEMU properties and devices, Goldfish CPU, build fingerprint, sensor count) |
| MASVS-RESILIENCE-2 | The app implements anti-tampering mechanisms | `mobshield-detect-integrity` | `android.integrity.signature`, `android.integrity.apk_checksum`, `android.integrity.native_lib_checksum`, `android.store.installer` |
| MASVS-RESILIENCE-4 | The app implements anti-dynamic analysis techniques | `mobshield-detect-hooks`, `mobshield-detect-debugger`, `mobshield-detect-environment` | Hooks: `common.hook.*`, `android.hook.*` (Frida maps, port, threads, Xposed and LSPosed). Debugger: `android.debug.*` (TracerPid, ptrace, timing, debuggable flag, waiting for debugger). Automation: `android.automation.framework` |

## Not covered

- **MASVS-RESILIENCE-3** (anti-static analysis: obfuscation and similar). Use your build toolchain
  (R8/ProGuard and dedicated obfuscators). MobShield does not obfuscate your code.
- **MASVS-RESILIENCE-1 beyond the signals above.** There is no device attestation in this
  library. Combine it with a server-verified attestation service for that.

## Using the mapping

Each signal's name, technique, default weight and confidence are listed in the per-module pages
under [docs/detection](detection). The [validation matrix](validation.md) shows what each
module reports on a clean device versus a compromised one, which is the evidence to cite when
documenting a control.

```

`docs/report-attestation.md`:

```md
# Verifying reports on your server

MobShield's report tells your backend what the app saw on the device. This page covers how to make
that report something a server can check, what the server must verify, and what a verified report
still cannot tell you.

## Why the HMAC-signed report is deprecated

`MobShield.currentSignedReport(key)` signs the report with HMAC-SHA256 under a key the app supplies.
That key has to ship inside the app, so anyone who can unpack or hook the app has it and can sign a
clean report for any device. The report also had no server challenge, and its timestamp comes from
the client, so a clean report captured once could be sent again forever. The signature only showed
that the report was not changed in transit.

`currentSignedReport` and `MobShieldReporter.sign` still work but are deprecated. Use one of the two
flows below.

## The flow

1. **Your server issues a challenge.** 16 to 128 random bytes (32 is a good size) from a secure
   random generator, stored server-side with a short expiry (a minute or two) and bound to the
   session or user that asked for it.
2. **The app builds a report that carries the challenge** and has it vouched for by the platform,
   using Key Attestation or Play Integrity (below).
3. **Your server verifies it**, and accepts the challenge **once**: delete it as soon as a report
   answers it, and reject reports for challenges that are unknown, expired, already used, or issued
   to a different session.

The challenge is in the report's canonical JSON (`"challenge"`, Base64), and the JSON is what gets
signed, so a report cannot be moved to another challenge. Ignore `generatedAtMs` for freshness: the
client sets it. Use the time your server issued the challenge instead.

## Option A: Key Attestation

Works without Google Play services and needs no Google API on your server.

```kotlin
// Off the main thread: creating a hardware-backed key can take seconds.
val challenge: ByteArray = api.fetchReportChallenge()
val report: AttestedThreatReport = withContext(Dispatchers.IO) {
    MobShield.currentAttestedReport(challenge)
}
api.sendReport(report.json, report.signature, report.certificateChain)
```

For each report, `AndroidKeystoreReportAttester` creates a new EC P-256 key in the Android Keystore
(StrongBox when the device has one, otherwise the TEE) with the challenge as its attestation
challenge, signs the report's canonical JSON with it, returns the key's certificate chain, and deletes
the key. It throws `ReportAttestationException` if the device cannot create or use an attested key.

The server checks, in this order:

1. **The chain.** Each certificate is signed by the next, the root is one of
   [Google's attestation roots](https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/security-key-attestation#root_certificate),
   and no certificate is on the
   [revocation status list](https://android.googleapis.com/attestation/status). Use Google's
   [key attestation verification library](https://github.com/android/keyattestation) rather than
   writing your own parser; it handles certificate expiry and other cases custom verifiers miss.
2. **The attestation record** (extension `1.3.6.1.4.1.11129.2.1.17`, read from the certificate
   closest to the root that has it, not blindly from the leaf):
   - `attestationChallenge` equals the challenge you issued.
   - `attestationSecurityLevel` is `TrustedEnvironment` or `StrongBox`, never `Software`. Emulators
     and devices without hardware attestation produce `Software` records with a non-Google root.
   - `attestationApplicationId` names your package and your app signing certificate's SHA-256 digest.
   - `rootOfTrust` meets your policy, for example `verifiedBootState` is `Verified` and
     `deviceLocked` is true. A device with an unlocked bootloader, which most root methods need,
     shows up here, from hardware, whatever the app reports.
3. **The signature.** `signature` (Base64 DER ECDSA with SHA-256) verifies over the UTF-8 bytes of
   `json` with the public key of the first certificate in `certificateChain`.
4. **The challenge in `json`** equals the one you issued, then mark that challenge used.

Only after all of this, read the report's `threats` and `riskLevel`.

## Option B: Play Integrity

If you already use the Play Integrity API, bind its verdict to the report instead:

```kotlin
val challenge: ByteArray = api.fetchReportChallenge()
val json = MobShieldReporter.canonicalJson(MobShield.currentReport(challenge))
// integrityTokenProvider comes from StandardIntegrityManager.prepareIntegrityToken(...).
val token = integrityTokenProvider.request(
    StandardIntegrityManager.StandardIntegrityTokenRequest.builder()
        .setRequestHash(MobShieldReporter.requestHash(json))
        .build(),
).await().token() // await() from kotlinx-coroutines-play-services
api.sendReport(json, token)
```

The server decodes the token with Google's `decodeIntegrityToken` endpoint, checks that
`requestDetails.requestHash` equals the lowercase hex SHA-256 of the `json` it received, checks
`appIntegrity` and `deviceIntegrity` against your policy, checks the challenge in `json` as in the
flow above, and only then reads the report. MobShield does not depend on the Play Integrity library;
add it to your app if you use this option.

## What a verified report proves, and what it does not

A verified report proves that it was produced **for your challenge**, **once**, by **your app**
(package and signing certificate), on a device whose **hardware** vouched for it, and that it was not
changed afterwards.

It does not prove that the report's **contents** are true. The app builds the report, and on a device
where an attacker can hook the app, the attacker can hand a clean report to the attested key or to
Play Integrity, which will vouch for it. No client-side signing scheme can prevent that. Therefore:

- Base decisions on what the **platform** attests: the boot state and security level in the
  attestation record, or Play Integrity's device and app verdicts. Those come from hardware or from
  Google, not from app code.
- Treat MobShield's `threats` as additional context: a report of a hook is strong evidence, but the
  absence of one is not proof that there is none.
- Key attestation can be defeated by leaked attestation keys, which is why the revocation list check
  matters, and why devices that launched with Android 16 or later use Remote Key Provisioning.

## Changes in this version

- New `MobShield.currentAttestedReport(challenge)`, `MobShieldReporter.attest`, `ReportAttester` and
  `AndroidKeystoreReportAttester`, `AttestedThreatReport`, and `ReportAttestationException`.
- New `MobShield.currentReport(challenge)` and `MobShieldReporter.requestHash(json)` for Play
  Integrity.
- Reports gain an optional `challenge` field; the schema version is now `report-2`. Reports built
  without a challenge are unchanged apart from the version.
- `currentSignedReport`, `MobShieldReporter.sign` and HMAC signing are deprecated.

```

`docs/scoring.md`:

```md
# How MobShield scores threats

This page describes how signals become threat scores and severities, what the default numbers mean,
and what they do not mean. The Android and iOS libraries implement the same model and share the
same default thresholds and correlation groups.

## From signals to a score

Each detector check emits a **signal** with a name, a **weight** (0-100, how serious the finding is
if true) and a **confidence** (0-100, how sure the check is). Signals are routed to a threat type by
name prefix (for example `android.root.*` and `ios.jb.*` go to `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS`; any name
containing `.hook.` goes to `HOOK_FRAMEWORK`). Names that match no prefix are ignored.

For each threat type, the score is computed like this:

1. Each signal contributes `weight x confidence / 100` points.
2. Signals that share a **correlation group** are combined with noisy-OR:
   `100 x (1 - (1 - a/100) x (1 - b/100) x ...)`. A group is never lower than its strongest member
   and never higher than the plain sum of its members.
3. Groups and ungrouped signals are added together.
4. The total is capped at 100 and truncated to an integer (80.75 becomes 80).

The arithmetic is done in integer hundredths of a point, rounding the noisy-OR complement up at
each step so a group is never overstated. Results are exact and identical on both platforms.

### Why correlation groups exist

Adding signals treats them as independent observations. Some are not: two checks may read the same
file, or detect the same tool through its different artifacts. Adding them counts one finding
several times. For example, `common.hook.frida_maps` and `android.hook.suspicious_library` both
scan `/proc/self/maps` for the same Frida and Xposed library names; when both fire, the plain sum
was 99 even though only one piece of evidence was observed.

Noisy-OR keeps corroboration (two checks agreeing still raises the score) but with diminishing
returns. Signals that are not in a group still add, exactly as before.

| Group | Signals | What they share |
|---|---|---|
| `hook.frida` | `common.hook.frida_maps`, `common.hook.prologue`, `android.hook.frida_port`, `android.hook.thread_name`, `android.hook.suspicious_library`, `ios.hook.frida_thread`, `ios.hook.frida_symbols`, `ios.hook.frida_port` | Artifacts of one Frida agent, gadget or server. `frida_maps` and `suspicious_library` read the same `/proc/self/maps` lines. |
| `hook.xposed` | `android.hook.art_dex`, `android.hook.xposed`, `android.hook.stack_leak` | The Xposed / LSPosed bridge, seen through the class loader and stack traces. |
| `root.mounts` | `android.root.mount_namespace`, `android.root.overlayfs` | The kernel mount tables (`/proc/1/mountinfo`, `/proc/self/mountinfo`, `/proc/self/mounts`). |
| `root.su_paths` | `android.root.path_probe`, `android.root.errno_deviation` | Probes of the same `su` and root paths. |
| `debug.tracer` | `android.debug.tracerpid`, `android.debug.ptrace` | One attached tracer: `TracerPid` in `/proc/self/status`, and `PTRACE_TRACEME` failing because the process is already traced. |
| `jb.dyld_images` | `ios.jb.dyld_image`, `ios.jb.dyld_header` | The same list of loaded images, read by name and by Mach-O header. |

`common.hook.prologue` detects inline hooks from any framework, not only Frida. It is in the Frida
group because Frida instruments through inline hooks, so the two move together when Frida is
present; on its own it scores exactly as before.

Example: Frida maps (75/85) plus Frida threads (70/80) used to add to 100. As a group they score 84,
which is still CRITICAL for `HOOK_FRAMEWORK` (critical at 65).

You can replace the table with `signalGroups` in the configuration (signal name to any group id).
An empty map turns grouping off and restores plain addition.

## From score to severity

Each threat type has a warning threshold *W* and an optional critical threshold *C*:

| Condition | Severity |
|---|---|
| score >= *C* | CRITICAL |
| score >= *W* | HIGH |
| score >= *W* / 2 | MEDIUM |
| score >= *W* / 4 | LOW |
| otherwise | INFO (not emitted) |

Division is integer division. `DEVELOPER_MODE` and `ADB_ENABLED` are informational: they have no
critical threshold, report HIGH at or above *W* and INFO below it, and are dropped below *W* / 2.

| Threat type | Warning | Critical |
|---|---|---|
| `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS` (root / jailbreak) | 40 | 70 |
| `HOOK_FRAMEWORK` | 35 | 65 |
| `DEBUGGER` | 30 | 60 |
| `EMULATOR` | 25 | 55 |
| `AUTOMATION` | 30 | 60 |
| `APP_INTEGRITY` | 50 | 80 |
| `UNOFFICIAL_STORE` | 20 | 50 |
| `DEVELOPER_MODE`, `ADB_ENABLED` | 15 | none |

## What one signal can do on its own

A critical score means different things for different threat types. Some single checks reach
CRITICAL by themselves; for others, CRITICAL needs several signals to agree. This table lists each
default Android signal when it is the only signal of its threat type. It is enforced by the
`SingleSignalSeverityTest` in each detector module, so it cannot drift from the code.

| Signal | Weight / confidence | Score | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Root** (`PRIVILEGED_ACCESS`, warning 40, critical 70) | | | |
| `android.root.mount_namespace` | 75 / 85 | 63 | HIGH |
| `android.root.kernelsu_sysfs` | 75 / 85 | 63 | HIGH |
| `android.root.magisk_uds` | 70 / 80 | 56 | HIGH |
| `android.root.zygisk_maps` | 70 / 80 | 56 | HIGH |
| `android.root.overlayfs` | 65 / 75 | 48 | HIGH |
| `android.root.errno_deviation` | 60 / 70 | 42 | HIGH |
| `android.root.dangerous_packages` | 55 / 70 | 38 | MEDIUM |
| `android.root.path_probe` | 25 / 35 | 8 | no event |
| `android.root.props` | 20 / 30 | 6 | no event |
| **Hooks** (`HOOK_FRAMEWORK`, warning 35, critical 65) | | | |
| `android.hook.frida_port` | 85 / 95 | 80 | CRITICAL |
| `common.hook.prologue` | 80 / 90 | 72 | CRITICAL |
| `common.hook.frida_maps` | 75 / 85 | 63 | HIGH |
| `android.hook.art_dex` | 75 / 85 | 63 | HIGH |
| `android.hook.thread_name` | 70 / 80 | 56 | HIGH |
| `android.hook.xposed` | 70 / 80 | 56 | HIGH |
| `android.hook.stack_leak` | 65 / 75 | 48 | HIGH |
| `android.hook.suspicious_library` | 55 / 65 | 35 | HIGH |
| **Debugger** (`DEBUGGER`, warning 30, critical 60) | | | |
| `android.debug.waiting` | 90 / 95 | 85 | CRITICAL |
| `android.debug.tracerpid` | 85 / 90 | 76 | CRITICAL |
| `android.debug.ptrace` | 80 / 85 | 68 | CRITICAL |
| `android.debug.timing` | 65 / 70 | 45 | HIGH |
| `android.debug.app_debuggable` | 70 / 60 | 42 | HIGH |
| `android.adb.enabled` (`ADB_ENABLED`) | 30 / 40 | 12 | INFO |
| **Emulator and automation** (`EMULATOR` 25/55, `AUTOMATION` 30/60) | | | |
| `android.env.qemu_device` | 28 / 75 | 21 | MEDIUM |
| `android.automation.framework` | 30 / 70 | 21 | MEDIUM |
| `android.env.cpu_goldfish` | 24 / 70 | 16 | MEDIUM |
| `android.env.qemu_props` | 22 / 65 | 14 | MEDIUM |
| `android.env.build_fingerprint` | 20 / 60 | 12 | MEDIUM |
| `android.env.sensor_count` | 18 / 55 | 9 | LOW |
| **Integrity and store** (`APP_INTEGRITY` 50/80, `UNOFFICIAL_STORE` 20/50) | | | |
| `android.integrity.signature` | 90 / 95 | 85 | CRITICAL |
| `android.integrity.apk_checksum` | 85 / 90 | 76 | HIGH |
| `android.integrity.native_lib_checksum` | 80 / 85 | 68 | HIGH |
| `android.store.installer` | 25 / 45 | 11 | MEDIUM |

What this means in practice:

- **Root never reaches CRITICAL from one signal.** The strongest single root signals score 63. Two
  independent root signals are needed, for example Magisk's socket (56) plus a mount-namespace
  difference (63), which adds to 100.
- **A hook or debugger can reach CRITICAL from one signal**: the Frida port, an inline-hook
  prologue change, a tracer, or the app waiting for a debugger.
- **No single emulator signal reaches HIGH**, so a developer's emulator stays below the warning
  threshold unless several emulator checks agree.
- **A wrong signing certificate is CRITICAL on its own**; a changed APK or native-library checksum is
  HIGH.
- **Only a signal rated HIGH or CRITICAL here can trigger termination.** Weak hints that add up to
  HIGH or CRITICAL are reported but never end the process; see [termination.md](termination.md).

This asymmetry is a consequence of the default weights, not a calibrated judgment (see below). If it
does not suit your app, change `thresholds` or `detectionTuning`.

## What the numbers are, and are not

- **The weights and confidences are hand-assigned defaults.** They have not been fitted to data,
  and the repository does not record how each value was chosen.
- **No false-positive or detection rates are published.** The project does not yet have
  measurements across real devices, vendor ROMs, root and jailbreak tools, or Frida setups.
- **A score is not a probability.** "Score 70" does not mean "70% likely to be rooted". Severities
  are bands chosen to fit the default weights.
- **Correlation groups are also judgment.** They are based on what each check reads, documented
  above, but the strength of the correlation has not been measured.

Treat the defaults as a starting point. Before you act on a severity (in particular before enabling
a termination policy), observe the events your own users produce and adjust `thresholds`,
`detectionTuning` or `signalGroups`.

### Help calibrate them

Reports from real devices are the only way these numbers can improve. Useful reports include:

- device model, OS version and ROM or build;
- the root, jailbreak or instrumentation tool and its version, or "clean device";
- the signals emitted (names, scores and severities from `onThreat`);
- whether the result was correct.

Open an issue with these details. Report a detection **bypass** privately through the repository's
security policy instead.

## Changes in this version

- Correlated signals are combined with noisy-OR instead of added (`signalGroups`, on by default).
- Scores are computed with exact integer arithmetic; a score that is a whole number can no longer be
  truncated one point low by floating-point error.
- `signalSetVersion` is now `signals-2026.11.0`, so a backend can tell which scoring produced a
  report.

```

`docs/termination.md`:

```md
# Process termination

MobShield runs in detect-only mode by default: it reports threats and never ends your process. This
page covers what happens when you opt in to termination, why the rules are what they are, and what
termination can and cannot do for you.

## When the process is terminated

With `detectOnly(false)` and a `TerminationPolicy` other than `NONE`, a scan ends the process when
any threat event meets **all** of these conditions:

1. **Severity.** `EXIT_ON_BYPASS` needs HIGH or CRITICAL; `EXIT_ON_CRITICAL` needs CRITICAL.
2. **Corroboration.** At least one of the event's signals reaches HIGH **on its own**, scored as if
   it were the only signal of its threat type.
3. **Type.** The event's threat type is in `terminateOn` (default: every type).

Your listener receives every event and `onAllChecksFinished` before the process is terminated.
Events that fail condition 2 or 3 are still reported exactly as before; they just cannot end the
process.

## Why corroboration is required

A threat's score adds up evidence from several checks (see [scoring.md](scoring.md)). That is useful
for reporting, but it means weak hints can reach a severity none of them reaches alone. For example,
three weak root hints a custom ROM can trip:

| Signal | Score alone |
|---|---|
| `android.root.dangerous_packages` | 38 (MEDIUM) |
| `android.root.path_probe` | 8 (no event) |
| `android.root.props` | 6 (no event) |
| **Together** | **53 (HIGH)** |

Before this change, `EXIT_ON_BYPASS` killed the app on that combination. None of these checks is
evidence of an active attack by itself, and the weights are not calibrated against real devices, so
a user on an unusual but legitimate device could be locked out of the app. Now the event is still
reported as HIGH, but it terminates only if a signal such as `android.root.mount_namespace` (63) is
also present.

## What can terminate, with the default weights

The single-signal table in [scoring.md](scoring.md#what-one-signal-can-do-on-its-own) shows each
signal's severity on its own; a signal rated HIGH or CRITICAL there can corroborate termination. In
practice:

- **Hooks, debugger, app integrity.** Every default signal is HIGH or CRITICAL on its own, so these
  terminate as before.
- **Root.** Six signals are strong on their own (mount namespace, KernelSU, Magisk socket, Zygisk,
  overlayfs, errno deviation). `EXIT_ON_CRITICAL` still needs a score of 70, which takes at least two
  root signals, one of them strong.
- **Emulator, automation, unofficial store.** No default signal reaches HIGH on its own, so **these
  types never terminate with the default weights.** They are still reported. If you need them to
  terminate, raise a signal's weight or confidence with `detectionTuning` so that it reaches HIGH
  alone, and accept the false-positive risk that comes with it.
- **ADB.** The ADB signal is informational and never terminates.

## Leaving a threat type out

Use `terminateOn` to keep reporting a type without ever terminating for it. Apps whose users may run
rooted or custom-ROM devices for legitimate reasons can exclude privileged access:

```kotlin
val config = MobShieldConfig.builder()
    .detectOnly(false)
    .terminationPolicy(TerminationPolicy.EXIT_ON_CRITICAL)
    .terminateOn(ThreatType.entries.toSet() - ThreatType.PRIVILEGED_ACCESS)
    .build()
```

## Termination is not a security boundary

Termination is a single call, `android.os.Process.killProcess`. Anyone able to hook the app, which is
exactly what Frida and Xposed do, can replace that call with a no-op, or suppress the checks that
would have triggered it. Termination therefore raises the cost of casual tampering; it does not stop a
determined attacker, and it should not be the only thing between an attacker and something valuable.

Make decisions that matter on your server. Everything the client sends, including MobShield's
reports, can be altered by the same attacker who can hook the app, so base those decisions on
platform attestation (the [Play Integrity API](https://developer.android.com/google/play/integrity))
and use MobShield's reports as additional context.

## Choosing a policy

1. Ship in detect-only mode first and look at what your real users' devices report.
2. If you then want termination, prefer `EXIT_ON_CRITICAL`, and limit `terminateOn` to the types
   whose false positives you can accept.
3. Use `EXIT_ON_BYPASS` only if locking out some legitimate users is acceptable for your app. It acts
   on HIGH events, which the corroboration rule makes safer but which still rest on uncalibrated
   weights.

## Changes in this version

- Termination requires a signal that reaches HIGH on its own (corroboration). Weak hints that only
  add up to HIGH or CRITICAL no longer end the process.
- New `terminateOn` option, defaulting to every threat type.
- With the default weights, emulator, automation and unofficial-store events no longer terminate.

```

`docs/validation.md`:

```md
# MobShield Android — Detection validation

This document defines **what each detector is expected to report**, so a run on a real device can
be checked against a known baseline instead of a guess. It is the reference for the validation
harness (`MobShieldValidation`) and any on-device procedure.

## Why this exists

Unit tests run on the JVM and cannot be rooted or hooked; instrumented tests run on an emulator or
a clean device. They prove the detectors do not crash and do not raise false positives in a clean
environment — they do **not** prove a detector fires when the threat is actually present. Confirming
positive detection requires running on a compromised device. This matrix + the harness make that
check repeatable.

## The validation harness

`MobShieldValidation` (in `mobshield-core`) runs the registered detection modules and returns a
`ValidationReport` exposing both layers:

- **Raw signals** — per module, exactly which `Signal`s fired, with weight/confidence/evidence.
- **Aggregated events** — the `ThreatEvent`s those signals map to, with severity and score.

```kotlin
// Register the modules you want to validate, then:
val report = MobShieldValidation.runRegistered()
report.modules.forEach { m -> println("${m.moduleName} fired=${m.didFire} ${m.signals.map { it.name }}") }
println("threats=${report.activeThreatTypes} severity=${report.highestSeverity}")
```

`report.firedSignalNames` and `report.activeThreatTypes` are the two things to compare against the
tables below. Exact weights/confidences live in each module's `*SignalDefaults.kt` (overridable via
`MobShieldConfig.detectionTuning`).

## Expected-signal matrix

### Root — module `root`, threat `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS`
`android.root.mount_namespace`, `android.root.magisk_uds`, `android.root.overlayfs`,
`android.root.errno_deviation`, `android.root.zygisk_maps`, `android.root.kernelsu_sysfs`,
`android.root.path_probe`, `android.root.dangerous_packages`, `android.root.props`

### Hooks — module `hooks`, threat `HOOK_FRAMEWORK`
`common.hook.frida_maps`, `common.hook.prologue`, `android.hook.frida_port`,
`android.hook.thread_name`, `android.hook.art_dex`, `android.hook.suspicious_library`,
`android.hook.xposed`, `android.hook.stack_leak`

### Debugger — module `debugger`, threat `DEBUGGER` (plus `ADB_ENABLED`)
`android.debug.tracerpid`, `android.debug.ptrace`, `android.debug.timing`,
`android.debug.app_debuggable`, `android.debug.waiting`, `android.adb.enabled` (→ `ADB_ENABLED`)

### Environment — module `environment`, threats `EMULATOR` / `AUTOMATION`
`android.env.qemu_props`, `android.env.qemu_device`, `android.env.cpu_goldfish`,
`android.env.build_fingerprint`, `android.env.sensor_count`,
`android.automation.framework` (→ `AUTOMATION`)

### Integrity — module `integrity`, threats `APP_INTEGRITY` / `UNOFFICIAL_STORE`
`android.integrity.signature`, `android.integrity.apk_checksum`,
`android.integrity.native_lib_checksum`, `android.integrity.native_self_check`,
`android.store.installer` (→ `UNOFFICIAL_STORE`)

## Environment baselines

### Clean environment (stock/emulator, Play install or debug build)
- **Must NOT fire:** any `android.root.*` or `.hook.*`. `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS` and `HOOK_FRAMEWORK`
  must be absent — the strong false-positive guards.
- **Situational:** `android.debug.*` fires when a debugger/ADB is attached (e.g. launching from
  Android Studio); `android.env.*` fires on an **emulator** (expected — the emulator *is* an
  emulator); `android.store.installer` fires for non-Play installs (sideloaded/debug); integrity
  signals fire only when their anchors (`expectedSigners`, `expectedApkSha256`,
  `expectedNativeLibSha256`) are configured. Validate the clean baseline on a physical Play install
  launched without a debugger.

### Compromised environment (expected positive detections)
| Scenario | Expect fired signals | Expect threat |
|---|---|---|
| Rooted (Magisk / KernelSU / Zygisk) | ≥1 of `android.root.*` | `PRIVILEGED_ACCESS` |
| `frida-server` / gadget / LSPosed / Xposed | ≥1 of `common.hook.frida_maps`, `android.hook.frida_port`, `android.hook.thread_name`, `android.hook.xposed` | `HOOK_FRAMEWORK` |
| Debugger / ADB attached | ≥1 of `android.debug.tracerpid`, `android.debug.ptrace` | `DEBUGGER` |
| Re-signed / repackaged APK (anchors configured) | `android.integrity.signature` and/or `android.integrity.apk_checksum` | `APP_INTEGRITY` |
| Tampered native core | `android.integrity.native_self_check` | `APP_INTEGRITY` (critical) |

```

`gradlew`:

```
#!/bin/sh

#
# Copyright © 2015-2021 the original authors.
#
# Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
# you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
# You may obtain a copy of the License at
#
#      https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0
#
# Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
# distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
# WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
# See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
# limitations under the License.
#

##############################################################################
#
#   Gradle start up script for POSIX generated by Gradle.
#
#   Important for running:
#
#   (1) You need a POSIX-compliant shell to run this script. If your /bin/sh is
#       noncompliant, but you have some other compliant shell such as ksh or
#       bash, then to run this script, type that shell name before the whole
#       command line, like:
#
#           ksh Gradle
#
#       Busybox and similar reduced shells will NOT work, because this script
#       requires all of these POSIX shell features:
#         * functions;
#         * expansions «$var», «${var}», «${var:-default}», «${var+SET}»,
#           «${var#prefix}», «${var%suffix}», and «$( cmd )»;
#         * compound commands having a testable exit status, especially «case»;
#         * various built-in commands including «command», «set», and «ulimit».
#
#   Important for patching:
#
#   (2) This script targets any POSIX shell, so it avoids extensions provided
#       by Bash, Ksh, etc; in particular arrays are avoided.
#
#       The "traditional" practice of packing multiple parameters into a
#       space-separated string is a well documented source of bugs and security
#       problems, so this is (mostly) avoided, by progressively accumulating
#       options in "$@", and eventually passing that to Java.
#
#       Where the inherited environment variables (DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS,
#       and GRADLE_OPTS) rely on word-splitting, this is performed explicitly;
#       see the in-line comments for details.
#
#       There are tweaks for specific operating systems such as AIX, CygWin,
#       Darwin, MinGW, and NonStop.
#
#   (3) This script is generated from the Groovy template
#       https://github.com/gradle/gradle/blob/HEAD/subprojects/plugins/src/main/resources/org/gradle/api/internal/plugins/unixStartScript.txt
#       within the Gradle project.
#
#       You can find Gradle at https://github.com/gradle/gradle/.
#
##############################################################################

# Attempt to set APP_HOME

# Resolve links: $0 may be a link
app_path=$0

# Need this for daisy-chained symlinks.
while
    APP_HOME=${app_path%"${app_path##*/}"}  # leaves a trailing /; empty if no leading path
    [ -h "$app_path" ]
do
    ls=$( ls -ld "$app_path" )
    link=${ls#*' -> '}
    case $link in             #(
      /*)   app_path=$link ;; #(
      *)    app_path=$APP_HOME$link ;;
    esac
done

# This is normally unused
# shellcheck disable=SC2034
APP_BASE_NAME=${0##*/}
# Discard cd standard output in case $CDPATH is set (https://github.com/gradle/gradle/issues/25036)
APP_HOME=$( cd "${APP_HOME:-./}" > /dev/null && pwd -P ) || exit

# Use the maximum available, or set MAX_FD != -1 to use that value.
MAX_FD=maximum

warn () {
    echo "$*"
} >&2

die () {
    echo
    echo "$*"
    echo
    exit 1
} >&2

# OS specific support (must be 'true' or 'false').
cygwin=false
msys=false
darwin=false
nonstop=false
case "$( uname )" in                #(
  CYGWIN* )         cygwin=true  ;; #(
  Darwin* )         darwin=true  ;; #(
  MSYS* | MINGW* )  msys=true    ;; #(
  NONSTOP* )        nonstop=true ;;
esac

CLASSPATH=$APP_HOME/gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar


# Determine the Java command to use to start the JVM.
if [ -n "$JAVA_HOME" ] ; then
    if [ -x "$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java" ] ; then
        # IBM's JDK on AIX uses strange locations for the executables
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java
    else
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/bin/java
    fi
    if [ ! -x "$JAVACMD" ] ; then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is set to an invalid directory: $JAVA_HOME

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
else
    JAVACMD=java
    if ! command -v java >/dev/null 2>&1
    then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is not set and no 'java' command could be found in your PATH.

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
fi

# Increase the maximum file descriptors if we can.
if ! "$cygwin" && ! "$darwin" && ! "$nonstop" ; then
    case $MAX_FD in #(
      max*)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -H is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        MAX_FD=$( ulimit -H -n ) ||
            warn "Could not query maximum file descriptor limit"
    esac
    case $MAX_FD in  #(
      '' | soft) :;; #(
      *)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -n is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        ulimit -n "$MAX_FD" ||
            warn "Could not set maximum file descriptor limit to $MAX_FD"
    esac
fi

# Collect all arguments for the java command, stacking in reverse order:
#   * args from the command line
#   * the main class name
#   * -classpath
#   * -D...appname settings
#   * --module-path (only if needed)
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and GRADLE_OPTS environment variables.

# For Cygwin or MSYS, switch paths to Windows format before running java
if "$cygwin" || "$msys" ; then
    APP_HOME=$( cygpath --path --mixed "$APP_HOME" )
    CLASSPATH=$( cygpath --path --mixed "$CLASSPATH" )

    JAVACMD=$( cygpath --unix "$JAVACMD" )

    # Now convert the arguments - kludge to limit ourselves to /bin/sh
    for arg do
        if
            case $arg in                                #(
              -*)   false ;;                            # don't mess with options #(
              /?*)  t=${arg#/} t=/${t%%/*}              # looks like a POSIX filepath
                    [ -e "$t" ] ;;                      #(
              *)    false ;;
            esac
        then
            arg=$( cygpath --path --ignore --mixed "$arg" )
        fi
        # Roll the args list around exactly as many times as the number of
        # args, so each arg winds up back in the position where it started, but
        # possibly modified.
        #
        # NB: a `for` loop captures its iteration list before it begins, so
        # changing the positional parameters here affects neither the number of
        # iterations, nor the values presented in `arg`.
        shift                   # remove old arg
        set -- "$@" "$arg"      # push replacement arg
    done
fi


# Add default JVM options here. You can also use JAVA_OPTS and GRADLE_OPTS to pass JVM options to this script.
DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS='-Dfile.encoding=UTF-8 "-Xmx64m" "-Xms64m"'

# Collect all arguments for the java command:
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and optsEnvironmentVar are not allowed to contain shell fragments,
#     and any embedded shellness will be escaped.
#   * For example: A user cannot expect ${Hostname} to be expanded, as it is an environment variable and will be
#     treated as '${Hostname}' itself on the command line.

set -- \
        "-Dorg.gradle.appname=$APP_BASE_NAME" \
        -classpath "$CLASSPATH" \
        org.gradle.wrapper.GradleWrapperMain \
        "$@"

# Stop when "xargs" is not available.
if ! command -v xargs >/dev/null 2>&1
then
    die "xargs is not available"
fi

# Use "xargs" to parse quoted args.
#
# With -n1 it outputs one arg per line, with the quotes and backslashes removed.
#
# In Bash we could simply go:
#
#   readarray ARGS < <( xargs -n1 <<<"$var" ) &&
#   set -- "${ARGS[@]}" "$@"
#
# but POSIX shell has neither arrays nor command substitution, so instead we
# post-process each arg (as a line of input to sed) to backslash-escape any
# character that might be a shell metacharacter, then use eval to reverse
# that process (while maintaining the separation between arguments), and wrap
# the whole thing up as a single "set" statement.
#
# This will of course break if any of these variables contains a newline or
# an unmatched quote.
#

eval "set -- $(
        printf '%s\n' "$DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS $JAVA_OPTS $GRADLE_OPTS" |
        xargs -n1 |
        sed ' s~[^-[:alnum:]+,./:=@_]~\\&~g; ' |
        tr '\n' ' '
    )" '"$@"'

exec "$JAVACMD" "$@"

```

`gradlew.bat`:

```bat
@rem
@rem Copyright 2015 the original author or authors.
@rem
@rem Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
@rem you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
@rem You may obtain a copy of the License at
@rem
@rem      https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0
@rem
@rem Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
@rem distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
@rem WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
@rem See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
@rem limitations under the License.
@rem

@if "%DEBUG%"=="" @echo off
@rem ##########################################################################
@rem
@rem  Gradle startup script for Windows
@rem
@rem ##########################################################################

@rem Set local scope for the variables with windows NT shell
if "%OS%"=="Windows_NT" setlocal

set DIRNAME=%~dp0
if "%DIRNAME%"=="" set DIRNAME=.
@rem This is normally unused
set APP_BASE_NAME=%~n0
set APP_HOME=%DIRNAME%

@rem Resolve any "." and ".." in APP_HOME to make it shorter.
for %%i in ("%APP_HOME%") do set APP_HOME=%%~fi

@rem Add default JVM options here. You can also use JAVA_OPTS and GRADLE_OPTS to pass JVM options to this script.
set DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS=-Dfile.encoding=UTF-8 "-Xmx64m" "-Xms64m"

@rem Find java.exe
if defined JAVA_HOME goto findJavaFromJavaHome

set JAVA_EXE=java.exe
%JAVA_EXE% -version >NUL 2>&1
if %ERRORLEVEL% equ 0 goto execute

echo. 1>&2
echo ERROR: JAVA_HOME is not set and no 'java' command could be found in your PATH. 1>&2
echo. 1>&2
echo Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the 1>&2
echo location of your Java installation. 1>&2

goto fail

:findJavaFromJavaHome
set JAVA_HOME=%JAVA_HOME:"=%
set JAVA_EXE=%JAVA_HOME%/bin/java.exe

if exist "%JAVA_EXE%" goto execute

echo. 1>&2
echo ERROR: JAVA_HOME is set to an invalid directory: %JAVA_HOME% 1>&2
echo. 1>&2
echo Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the 1>&2
echo location of your Java installation. 1>&2

goto fail

:execute
@rem Setup the command line

set CLASSPATH=%APP_HOME%\gradle\wrapper\gradle-wrapper.jar


@rem Execute Gradle
"%JAVA_EXE%" %DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS% %JAVA_OPTS% %GRADLE_OPTS% "-Dorg.gradle.appname=%APP_BASE_NAME%" -classpath "%CLASSPATH%" org.gradle.wrapper.GradleWrapperMain %*

:end
@rem End local scope for the variables with windows NT shell
if %ERRORLEVEL% equ 0 goto mainEnd

:fail
rem Set variable GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE if you need the _script_ return code instead of
rem the _cmd.exe /c_ return code!
set EXIT_CODE=%ERRORLEVEL%
if %EXIT_CODE% equ 0 set EXIT_CODE=1
if not ""=="%GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE%" exit %EXIT_CODE%
exit /b %EXIT_CODE%

:mainEnd
if "%OS%"=="Windows_NT" endlocal

:omega

```

        outputDir.mkdirs()

        val header =
            """
            |#ifndef MOBSHIELD_BUILDINFO_H
            |#define MOBSHIELD_BUILDINFO_H
            |#define MOBSHIELD_VERSION "$mobshieldVersion"
            |#define MOBSHIELD_BUILD_ID "$buildId"

            |#endif
            """.trimMargin()

    }
}

        targetCompatibility = JavaVersion.VERSION_17

        jvmTarget = "17"
    }

        buildConfig = true
    }

        unitTests.isIncludeAndroidResources = true
    }

    task.name.startsWith("configureCMake") || task.name.startsWith("buildCMake")

`mobshield-core/src/androidTest/kotlin/io/mobshield/core/AndroidKeystoreReportAttesterInstrumentedTest.kt`:

```kt
/*
 * Copyright 2025 MobShield Contributors
 *
 * Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
 * you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
 * You may obtain a copy of the License at
 *
 *     http://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0
 *
 * Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
 * distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
 * WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
 * See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
 * limitations under the License.
 */
package io.mobshield.core

import androidx.test.ext.junit.runners.AndroidJUnit4
import org.junit.Assert.assertFalse
import org.junit.Assert.assertNotNull
import org.junit.Assert.assertTrue
import org.junit.Test
import org.junit.runner.RunWith
import java.io.ByteArrayInputStream
import java.security.KeyStore
import java.security.Signature
import java.security.cert.CertificateFactory
import java.security.cert.X509Certificate

/**
 * Runs the real Keystore attester. On an emulator the chain is software-rooted, which a server must
 * reject, but the structure is the same: the leaf carries the Key Attestation extension containing
 * the challenge, and its key verifies the signature.
 */
@RunWith(AndroidJUnit4::class)
class AndroidKeystoreReportAttesterInstrumentedTest {
    private val challenge = ByteArray(32) { (it * 7).toByte() }
    private val payload = "{\"challenge\":\"test\"}".toByteArray()

    @Test
    fun attest_returnsAChainWhoseLeafSignedThePayloadAndCarriesTheChallenge() {
        val attestation = AndroidKeystoreReportAttester(preferStrongBox = false).attest(challenge, payload)

        val certificates = attestation.certificateChain.map(::certificate)
        assertTrue(certificates.size > 1)
        val leaf = certificates.first()

        val extension = leaf.getExtensionValue(KEY_ATTESTATION_OID)
        assertNotNull(extension)
        assertTrue(contains(extension!!, challenge))

        val verifier = Signature.getInstance("SHA256withECDSA")
        verifier.initVerify(leaf.publicKey)
        verifier.update(payload)
        assertTrue(verifier.verify(attestation.signature))
    }

    @Test
    fun attest_deletesTheKeyAfterwards() {
        AndroidKeystoreReportAttester(preferStrongBox = false).attest(challenge, payload)

        val keyStore = KeyStore.getInstance("AndroidKeyStore").apply { load(null) }
        assertFalse(keyStore.aliases().toList().any { it.startsWith("io.mobshield.report.") })
    }

    private fun certificate(der: ByteArray): X509Certificate =
        CertificateFactory.getInstance("X.509").generateCertificate(ByteArrayInputStream(der)) as X509Certificate

    private fun contains(
        haystack: ByteArray,
        needle: ByteArray,
    ): Boolean =
        (0..haystack.size - needle.size).any { start ->
            needle.indices.all { haystack[start + it] == needle[it] }
        }

    private companion object {
        const val KEY_ATTESTATION_OID = "1.3.6.1.4.1.11129.2.1.17"
    }
}

```

/*

#include <jni.h>

#include <cstring>

#include "mobshield_native.h"

extern "C" JNIEXPORT jint JNICALL JNI_OnLoad(JavaVM* vm, void* /*reserved*/) {
    JNIEnv* env = nullptr;
    if (vm->GetEnv(reinterpret_cast<void**>(&env), JNI_VERSION_1_6) != JNI_OK) {
        return JNI_ERR;
    }
    mobshield_native_init();
    return JNI_VERSION_1_6;
}

extern "C" JNIEXPORT jstring JNICALL
Java_io_mobshield_core_internal_NativeBridge_nativeGetVersion(JNIEnv* env, jclass /*clazz*/) {
    char buffer[64];
    if (mobshield_native_get_version(buffer, sizeof(buffer)) < 0) {
        return env->NewStringUTF("");
    }
    return env->NewStringUTF(buffer);
}

extern "C" JNIEXPORT jstring JNICALL
Java_io_mobshield_core_internal_NativeBridge_nativeGetBuildId(JNIEnv* env, jclass /*clazz*/) {
    char buffer[128];
    if (mobshield_native_get_build_id(buffer, sizeof(buffer)) < 0) {
        return env->NewStringUTF("");
    }
    return env->NewStringUTF(buffer);
}

extern "C" JNIEXPORT jint JNICALL
Java_io_mobshield_core_internal_NativeBridge_nativeSelfCheck(JNIEnv* /*env*/, jclass /*clazz*/) {
    return mobshield_native_self_check();
}
